**Step 1:** The testimonies from White (Testimony 8) state that “The victim ran to the left, and you gave chase!” . Evidence 9 (May Testimony) reports that “The victim dodged an attack then ran to the right, but she was caught and struck.” These two statements describe opposite directions for the victim’s movement at the same moment, creating a direct spatial contradiction.

**Step 2:** The generated spatial reasoning rules include Rule 2: *If XXX is to the left of YYY then YYY is to the right of XXX*. This rule captures the logical relationship between opposite directions.

**Step 3:** Applying Rule 2, the claim that the victim ran **left** (Testimony 8) implies that any reference to the victim’s position relative to the attacker would be **right** from the attacker’s perspective. Evidence 9 asserts the victim ran **right**, which is the opposite of what follows from Rule 2 given the testimony. Hence, the two sources cannot both be true.

**Step 4:** Even without the explicit rule, the contradiction is evident because a single event cannot simultaneously involve the victim moving left and right. The spatial inconsistency between the two accounts identifies the conflicting pair.

- Evidence 9 (May Testimony) states: “The victim dodged an attack then ran to the right, but she was caught and struck.” – indicating the victim moved rightward.  
These two statements describe opposite directions for the same action, creating a direct spatial contradiction.

Step 2: Check generated rules  
- Rule 2: “If XXX is to the left of YYY then YYY is to the right of XXX.” This rule captures the left/right relationship and shows that “left” and “right” are mutually exclusive for the same reference point. The two statements violate this rule because they assign opposite directions to the same movement.

Step 3: Apply the rule  
- Applying Rule 2, if Mia is “to the left” of the door (or any reference point) as White claims, then the door (or reference) would be “to the right” of Mia. May’s claim that Mia ran “to the right” contradicts this, because both cannot be true simultaneously for the same reference frame.

Step 4: Spatial reasoning without rules (if needed)  
Even without formal rules, the simple spatial fact that an object cannot simultaneously move left and right at the same moment establishes the contradiction.

**Step 1:** The crime photo (Evidence 2) shows the umbrella “lying open in a distance” from the victim, implying the umbrella is far away and not under the victim. Phoenix’s testimony (Testimony 3) states that after shoving the victim, “he fell down on top of his cheap umbrella,” meaning the umbrella was directly beneath the victim at the moment of the fall. These two statements place the umbrella in mutually exclusive locations relative to the victim.

**Step 2:** The spatial relationship described in the evidence (umbrella is distant from the victim) conflicts with the relationship described in the testimony (umbrella is directly under/adjacent to the victim).

**Step 3:** Rule 9 (“If XXX is adjacent to YYY then YYY is adjacent to XXX”) applies. The testimony asserts adjacency/overlap between victim and umbrella, while the photo evidence shows they are not adjacent (they are at a distance). The violation of the expected reciprocal adjacency reveals the contradiction.

**Step 4:** Even without the explicit rule, the contradiction is identified by comparing the spatial positions: “in a distance” vs. “on top of,” which cannot both be true simultaneously.
